The sheet hangs heavily, its weight visibly pulling the line down in a shallow curve. To her right, seated in a minimalist hot pink chair, is Zhao Ming, a Chinese man in his late thirties. His enlarged head, crowned with sleek black hair, tilts back as he inspects the hanging sheet from a distance of mere inches. One hand rests on his knee, the other extends a rigid index finger pointing directly at a perceived wrinkle in the fabric, his face a mask of smug, detached critique, his brows arched high. Behind Liu Jia, partially blocking the railing, stands Sun Lei, a Chinese man in his early thirties in a bold cobalt hoodie. His body is turned toward Liu Jia, his own massive, dinner-plate-sized hands clutching a second, equally large white sheet, ready to hand it off. However, his stance is unconsciously obstructive; his left foot, in a white sneaker, is planted squarely in front of a woven wicker basket overflowing with wooden clothespins, rendering them inaccessible to Liu Jia without her having to ask or maneuver around him. His jaw is set, his brows drawn down in a look of focused readiness that paradoxically creates the next hurdle. The balcony setting is ruthlessly clean: a stretch of emerald-painted wall, a glossy white railing, and the single taut clothesline forming the only geometry. The sharp morning light rakes across the scene, illuminating dust motes and deepening the shadows under the sagging sheet and behind the figures.
